SUPPLEMENTAL METHODS 
 
Cell Culture Experiments. 
Cell Viability Assay. Cell viability was assayed on a BioTek Synergy Neo2 using 
CellTiterGlo (Promega, Madison, WI, Cat. No. G7570) according to the manufacturer’s 
instructions. Briefly, cells were plated in a white-bottom 384 well plate (Corning Inc., Corning, 
NY) and treated with the indicated concentrations of fisetin, as described1. Viability was 
assessed 48hr after treatment and normalized to the DMSO control. IC50 values were calculated 
using GraphPad Prism. All values are the mean of 4-8 replicate measurements.  
Senescence-associated β-galactosidase (SA-β-gal) staining. SA-β-gal staining was 
performed using the Senescence Detection Kit (Abcam, Cambridge, UK, Cat. No. ab65351) 
according to the manufacturer’s instructions. Briefly, after overnight seeding, late passaged 
human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVECs) were treated with 1μM fisetin in media for 
48hr. Following the incubation period, early passaged (control), late passaged (senescent), and 
senescent cells treated with 1μM were washed, fixed, and incubated with the X-gal solution 
overnight. Images were captured using bright-field microscopy at 20X magnification and 
quantified using ImageJ, as described2. 
Assessment of Reactive Oxygen Species (ROS) levels. Oxidative stress was detected in 
cells using the CellROX Deep Red ROS probe (Invitrogen, Waltham, MA) according to the 
manufacturer’s instructions. Briefly, cells were plated in a high content image plate with 0.2mm 
glass bottom (Corning, Kennebunk, ME) coated with bovine gelatin substrate (Attachment 
Factor Solution; Cell Applications, Inc., San Diego, CA) to facilitate cell adhesion. After a 24hr 
incubation, attached cells were washed once with Hanks’ balanced salt solution (HBSS) and 
treated with 1μM fisetin for 4hr. Attached cells were then washed twice with HBSS and 

 
 
116 
incubated with 9.2μM CellROX Deep Red ROS probe for 60 minutes. After dye removal and 
three washes with HBSS, cells were left in 100μL of HBSS for subsequent imaging. Microscopy 
was carried out on an EVOS FL microscope (Invitrogen, Carlsbad, CA) set at 20X 
magnification. Cells were selected for imaging based on characteristic endothelial cell and 
nuclear morphology. Cell images were analyzed with Fiji (National Institutes of Health) and 
image intensity was averaged over the entire cell area3.  
Cell and Aortic Gene Expression. mRNA gene expression was measured in human 
aortic endothelial cells (HAECs) and segments of thoracic aorta following mechanical 
homogenization. RNA was extracted using the RNeasy mini kit (Qiagen, Hilden, Germany). 
cDNA was synthesized using the iScript cDNA synthesis kit (Bio-Rad Laboratories, Hercules, 
CA). Transcripts of cellular senescence and senescence-associated secretory phenotype (SASP) 
markers (primer sequences reported in Table S2) were analyzed using a StepOnePlus Real-Time 
PCR System (Applied Biosystems, Waltham, MA) in 96-well plates and the Taqman OpenArray 
(Applied Biosystems, Waltham, MA) was used as a master mix, as described4. SimpleSeq DNA 
sequencing (Quintara Biosciences, Cambridge, MA) was used to validate PCR products.  
Animal Experiments.  
Animals and Experimental Design. Male C57Bl/6N (wildtype) mice were obtained from 
the National Institute of Aging colony (maintained by Charles River, Wilmington, MA). 
Wildtype mice were allowed to acclimate to our facilities for 4 weeks before beginning the 
study. Male and female p16-3MR mice were bred and aged in our mouse colony at the 
University of Colorado Boulder. These mice carry a trimodal fusion protein (3MR) under the 
control of the p16INK4A promoter which allows for selective genetic clearance of p16INK4A-
positive (p16+) senescent cells by administering the antiviral agent ganciclovir (GCV)5. For the 

 
 
117 
duration of the study, all mice were single housed at the University of Colorado Boulder animal 
facility with a 12hr:12hr light-dark cycle and allowed ad libitum access to an irradiated, fixed, 
and open rodent chow (Inotiv/Envigo 7917, stored at room temperature). 
For the intervention period, wildtype and p16-3MR mice were randomly assigned to 
receive vehicle (10% Ethanol, 30% PEG400 and 60% Phosal 50 PG) or fisetin (100 mg/kg/day 
in vehicle). For wildtype mice, 18 mice received vehicle and 19 mice received fisetin, and for 
p16-3MR mice, 27 mice received vehicle and 35 mice received fisetin. Treatment was 
administered via oral gavage using an intermittent dosing paradigm – one week on; two weeks 
off; one week on. Mice were sacrificed one to two weeks following the final dose to rule out any 
acute effects of the compound as the terminal half-life of fisetin is ~3.1 hours in plasma6. 
Animals were administered vehicle or fisetin daily during the treatment period. Treatment groups 
were matched for baseline body weight and aortic PWV. Throughout this intervention period, 1 
old wildtype mouse and 11 p16-3MR mice died as a result of typical age-related attrition, which 
resulted in a final sample size of old Vehicle, n = 17; old Fisetin, n = 19; old 3MR Vehicle, n = 
22; and old 3MR Fisetin, n = 29.  
In Vivo Aortic Stiffness and Arterial Blood Pressure. Aortic stiffness was assessed 
using the reference standard non-invasive in vivo measure, aortic PWV, one week before (pre) 
and one week after (post) the intervention, as previously described7-10. Briefly, mice were placed 
under light isoflurane anesthesia (1.0-2.5%) and positioned supine on a warmed heat pad. Front- 
and hind-limb paws were then secured to corresponding ECG electrodes. Two Doppler probes 
were then placed on the skin at the transverse aortic arch and the abdominal aorta. Once clear R-
waves were registered, three repeated 2-second ultrasound tracings were recorded and average 
pre-ejection time (i.e., time between the R-wave of the ECG to the foot of the Doppler signal) 

 
 
118 
was determined for each location. To calculate aortic PWV, the distance between the two probes 
was divided by the difference between the transverse aortic arch and abdominal aorta pre-
ejection times (timeabdominal – timearch) and is reported as centimeters/second (cm/s). To examine 
the potential contribution of changes in arterial blood pressure to any treatment-related 
differences in aortic PWV, systolic and diastolic blood pressures were assessed using a CODA 
noninvasive tail-cuff system (Kent Scientific, Torrington, CT), as we have described previously7-
10. Briefly, the pressure measurements from 20 collection cycles (following 5 acclimation cycles) 
for 3 consecutive days were averaged per mouse at each timepoint. 
Vascular endothelial function: endothelium-dependent dilation (EDD), cellular 
senescence-mediated suppression of EDD, NO-mediated EDD, whole-cell and mitochondrial-
specific ROS suppression of EDD, and endothelium-independent dilation (EID). Upon 
completion of in vivo measures, mice were euthanized via cardiac exsanguination under inhaled 
isoflurane anesthesia, and carotid arteries were immediately excised. Vasodilatory function was 
measured via ex vivo carotid artery EDD and EID in response to increasing doses of 
acetylcholine (ACh) and sodium nitroprusside (SNP), respectively, as described previously 7-10. 
In brief, after vessels were pre-constricted with phenylephrine (PE; 2mM; Sigma-Aldrich, Cat. 
No. P6126), EDD was assessed by measuring increases in luminal diameter in response to 
increasing concentrations of ACh (1 X 10-9 to 1 X 10-4 M; Sigma-Aldrich, Cat. No. A6625) with 
and without ex vivo coadministration of GCV (5μm, 180min pre-incubation; Sigma Aldrich, St. 
Louis, MO, Cat. No. G2536), tonic ROS scavenger TEMPOL (1mM, 60min pre-incubation; 
Sigma-Aldrich, Cat. No. 2226-96-2), mitochondrial-specific ROS scavenger MitoQ (1µM, 
60min pre-incubation; Antipodean Pharmaceuticals, Inc., Menlo Park, CA, USA), or the NO 
synthase inhibitor NG-nitro-L-arginine methylester (L-NAME, 0.1mM, 30min pre-incubation; 

 
 
119 
Sigma-Aldrich, Cat. No. N5751). NO-mediated dilation was then calculated as the difference 
between maximum EDD to ACh alone and in the presence of ACh and L-NAME: 
NO-mediated dilation (%) = Maximum dilationACh - Maximum dilationACh+L-NAME 
Following EDD and pharamo-dissections of EDD, EID was assessed by measuring the 
increase in luminal diameter in response to increasing concentrations of SNP, an exogenous NO 
donor (1 X 10-10 to 1 X 10-4 M; Sigma-Aldrich, Cat. No. 13755-38-9). All dose response data are 
presented as percent dilation relative to maximum diameter to account for differences in baseline 
vessel diameter. 
Aortic ROS Production. Whole-cell aortic ROS production was assessed using the spin 
probe 1-hydroxy-3-methoxycarbonyl-2,2,5,5-tetramethylpyrrolidine (CMH; Enzo Life Sciences, 
Farmingdale, NY, Cat. No. ALX-430-078) and mitochondrial-specific ROS production was 
assessed using the spin probe 1-hydroxy-4-[2-triphenylphosphonio-acetamido]-2,2,6,6-
tetramethylpiperidine (mitoTEMPO-H; Enzo Life Sciences, Cat. No. ALX-430-171-M005) by 
electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR) spectrometry, as previously described 7-10. In short, two 
1-mm aortic rings were washed in warm physiological saline solution and incubated in 
Krebs/HEPES buffer, consisting of 99mM NaCl, 4.7mM KCl, 1.87mM CaCl2, 1.2mM MgSO4, 
25mM NaHCO3, 1.03mM KH2PO4, 20mM Na-HEPES, 11.1mM glucose, 0.1mM 
diethylenetriaminepenta-acetic acid, 0.0035mM sodium diethyldithiocarbamate, and Chelex 
(Sigma-Aldrich, Cat. No. C7901), containing 0.5mM CMH or mitoTEMPO-H at 37°C for 
60min. Samples were analyzed using aMS300 Xband EPR spectrometer (Magnettech, Berlin, 
Germany) with the following instrument parameters: B0-Field, 3350G; sweep, 80G; sweep time, 
60s; modulation, 3000mG; MWatten, 7dB; gain, 500. 

 
 
120 
Aortic Intrinsic Mechanical Wall Stiffness (Elastic Modulus) and Cellular Senescence-
Mediated Changes in Aortic Intrinsic Mechanical Wall Stiffness. Aortas were promptly 
excised from the mice following carotid artery excision, rinsed with cold physiological saline 
solution, and cleared of any remnant perivascular adipose and connective tissue. To measure ex 
vivo aortic stiffness, two thoracic aorta samples (~1 mm in length) were cut and used to 
determine intrinsic mechanical stiffness via pin myography as we have previously described7-9. 
In short, aorta samples were placed in heated (37°C) baths filled with calcium-free, phosphate-
buffered saline (PBS). The samples were then mounted on two wire prongs, followed by three 
rounds of pre-stretching. Once pre-stretching was complete, aortic ring diameter was increased 
until 1mN of force was reached and incrementally increased by 5 µm every 3 min thereafter until 
failure. The force corresponding to each stretching interval was recorded and used to calculate 
stress and strain. A stress-strain curve was then generated using the following equations: 
where d is diameter and di is initial diameter. 
where L is one-dimensional load, H is intima media thickness, and D is vessel length. 
The elastic modulus of the stress-strain curve was determined as the slope of the linear 
regression fit to the final four points of the stress-strain curve, as previously reported by our 
laboratory7-10. To assess the stiffening role of cellular senescence and the beneficial effects of 
fisetin on intrinsic mechanical stiffness, wildtype aorta rings were pre-incubated with 2.5µM 
ABT-263, a synthetic senolytic, and p16-3MR aortas were pre-incubated with 5 µM GCV, which 
are concentrations that have been shown to reduce the abundance of senescent cells ex vivo11,12, 
for 48hr prior to the following measurements. Aortic intima media thickness and diameter were 
assessed as we have described previously7-10. Briefly, aortic rings (1 mm) were frozen in optimal 
cutting temperature solution and stored at -80°C until the time of sectioning. Aortic sectioning 

 
 
121 
was performed on a cryostat (7 µm; Leica CM300, Leica Biosystems, Wetzlar, Germany) at -
22°C and sections were visualized, and images were captured with a bright-field microscope. 
Aortic intima media thickness and diameter were calculated using ImageJ software.  
Immunohistochemistry in Mouse Aortas. The localization of collagen-1 and advanced 
glycation end-products (AGEs) were assessed using ~1mm sections of mouse thoracic aorta 
which were collected at the time of sacrifice, frozen in OCT compound, and stored at -80°C until 
the time of sectioning. Samples were later sectioned (7 µm; Leica CM300, Leica Biosystems, 
Wetzlar, Germany) and plated in poly-L-lysine coated slides, fixed in 4% paraformaldehyde and 
stored at -80°C until further use in immunohistochemical analyses. Upon staining, the slides 
were rehydrated (PBS with 50 mM glycine), washed with PBS, permeabilized (0.1% Triton X-
100), and incubated for 20 minutes with 2.5% normal horse serum blocking buffer following 3 
washes with PBS. Slides were then incubated with primary antibodies at room temperature for 1 
hour, washed 3 times with PBS, incubated with horseradish peroxidase-conjugated secondary 
antibody for 30 minutes, treated with 1:1 3,3’-Diaminobenzidine (DAB):H2O2 for approximately 
30 minutes as the samples began to darken, and then immediately washed in PBS and cured 
overnight with gelvatol. Collagen-1 and AGEs primary antibodies (antibody descriptions 
reported in Table S3 were used for aortic protein targets, as previously described9,10. The slides 
were imaged using a Nikon Eclipse TS100 under identical conditional and analyzed using 
ImageJ.  
Aortic Protein Abundance. Protein abundance was measured in segments of thoracic 
aorta following mechanical homogenization in radioimmunoprecipitation assay lysis buffer 
supplemented with protease and phosphatase inhibitors (1mM sodium orthovanadate, 1X 
complete mini protease inhibitor cocktail tablet [Roche, Mannheim, Germany, Cat. No. 

 
 
122 
11836153001], 1 mM phenylmethylsulphonyl fluoride, 1:100 Phosphatase Inhibitor Cocktail 
[Sigma-Aldrich, Cat. No. P2850], 5mM sodium fluoride, and 5mM sodium pyrophosphate). 
Total protein content was quantified using a bicinchoninic acid assay (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 
Cat. No. 23225). Next, abundance of AGEs, α-elastin, collagen-1, p16INK4A, total and 
phosphorylated p66SHC, nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NAPDH) oxidase, CuZn 
superoxide dismutase (SOD), MnSOD, and Glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate dehydrogenase 
(GAPDH) (antibody descriptions reported in Table S3) were determined by loading 20ng/mL of 
aortic protein per capillary in a 25-lane (capillary) automated Western blot quantitative analyzer 
(WES, ProteinSimple, San Jose, CA), according to the manufacturer’s guidelines, as described 
previously 7-10, following the validation of these antibodies in test aorta lysates. Secondary 
antibodies were provided by the manufacturer and used according to the manufacturer’s 
guidelines. A grayscale analysis of the band intensities was then performed to quantify protein 
abundance using Compass software (ProteinSimple), with target proteins expressed relative to a 
loading control (GAPDH). Data files may be accessed at DOI: 10.6084/m9.figshare.24061176. 
Statistical Analyses. Power calculations were performed using G*power 3.1 (RRID: 
SCR_013726) for our primary outcome variable, aortic PWV. Previously, our laboratory has 
obtained effect sizes of 1.35 when comparing aortic PWV between treatment groups. With this 
effect size, N=6 mice per condition were required to achieve 99% statistical power. Additional 
mice were studied in each group to ensure sufficient PWV traces were obtained and to account 
for age-related attrition.   
 Statistical analyses were conducted using GraphPad Prism version 9.4.0 (GraphPad 
Software, Inc., San Diego, CA, USA; RRID:SCR_002798). Data were assessed for statistical 
outliers (ROUT test; Q = 1%), and outliers were excluded from final analyses. All variables were 

 
 
123 
assessed using unpaired t-test Statistical significance was set to α=0.05. Data are presented as 
mean ± SEM.

CHAPTER IV 
 
Manuscript in preparation, to be submitted for publication at Nature Aging 
 
Vascular Cell Senescence and Endothelial Dysfunction: The Role of the Senescence-
Associated Secretory Phenotype and CXCL12 
 
Sophia A. Mahoney1, Krystyna Mazan-Mamczarz2, Dimitrios Tsitsipatis2, Nicholas S. 
VanDongen1, Sanna Darvish1, Kevin O. Murray1, Douglas R. Seals1, Matthew J. Rossman1, 
Alison B. Herman2#, Zachary S. Clayton3# 
 
1 Department of Integrative Physiology, University of Colorado Boulder, Boulder, CO 
2 National Institute on Aging, Baltimore, MD 
3 Department of Medicine – Geriatrics, University of Colorado Anschutz Medical Campus, 
Aurora, CO 
 
# Authors contributed equally 
 
 
GRAPHICAL ABSTRACT 
 


 
 
133 
ABSTRACT 
 
Cellular senescence and the senescence-associated secretory phenotype (SASP) contribute to 
age-related endothelial dysfunction, in part, by promoting oxidative stress (a key source being 
mitochondria) and inflammation, which reduce the bioavailability of the vasodilatory molecule 
nitric oxide (NO). In the present study, we leveraged aortic single-cell transcriptomics to 
characterize specific vascular senescent cells and SASP profiles in young (6mo) vs. old (27mo) 
mice. In vivo supplementation with the natural senolytic compound fisetin allowed for the 
assessment of senescent cell signatures with aging and senolytic intervention. Both aging and 
senolytic treatment had the greatest effect on endothelial cell populations. As such, we focused 
on senescent-specific molecular changes in endothelial cells. Senescent endothelial cells 
demonstrated increased transcription of genes related to cardiovascular pathology and high 
expression of SASP factors. Of the identified SASP factors that had higher expression with 
aging, Cxcl12 was the most highly upregulated with old age and downregulated with senolytic 
treatment and this pattern persisted in circulating CXCL12 protein levels in plasma. We next 
sought to determine the causal role of the circulating SASP milieu and CXCL12 in regulating 
endothelial function with aging and fisetin supplementation. Ex vivo exposure of isolated arteries 
and cultured endothelial cells to plasma from young, old and old fisetin-supplemented mice 
revealed that the aged circulating SASP milieu directly induced endothelial dysfunction through 
induction of cellular senescence induction, reduced NO bioactivity, and increased mitochondrial 
oxidative stress. The circulating SASP milieu-mediated impairments were in part transduced by 
CXCL12 and prevented by fisetin supplementation. These findings establish the circulating 
SASP milieu as an underlying mechanism of age-related endothelial dysfunction and identify 

 
 
134 
CXCL12 as a novel mediator and therapeutic target of cellular senescence-induced endothelial 
dysfunction with aging.  
INTRODUCTION 
 
Advancing age is the primary risk factor for cardiovascular diseases (CVDs), which 
remain the leading cause of morbidity and mortality worldwide1,2. Vascular dysfunction, 
characterized in part by impaired vascular endothelial function, is a key antecedent to the 
development of clinical CVDs with aging3. Cellular senescence is an antagonistic cellular 
“hallmark” of aging that directly contributes to age-related vascular dysfunction4-7. Basal levels 
of senescent cells mediate stress responses that contribute to tumor suppression, wound healing 
and tissue regeneration5,7. However, states of excess cellular senescence promote the 
development of cancer, chronic inflammation and declines in physiological function and tissue 
aging5,7. Senescent cells elicit a multifactorial stress response, in part, via the production of the 
senescence-associated secretory phenotype (SASP)8. The SASP can induce cellular and tissue 
level dysfunction by driving excess superoxide-related oxidative stress (a key source being from 
the mitochondria), chronic inflammation, and induction of cellular senescence in neighboring 
cells/tissues (a process termed senescence-induced senescence)8. Unique to the vasculature, the 
SASP from senescent endothelial cells may have systemic effects given the direct contact 
between the endothelium and the circulating milieu (i.e., bioactive molecules in the 
bloodstream), a key regulator of endothelial function with aging9,10. Conversely, vascular 
endothelial cells may also be more susceptible to entering a senescent state given the constant 
interaction of the endothelium with the circulating SASP milieu9,11. However, vascular cell-
specific origins of senescent cells and the SASP are not well characterized. As such, gaining a 
greater understanding of the cellular senescence- and SASP-related molecular mechanisms may 

 
 
135 
be exploited as translational diagnostic biomarkers, and may serve as putative therapeutic targets 
for novel interventions. 
In addition to the SASP, characteristic properties of senescent cells include upregulation 
of the genes Cdkn1a and Cdkn2a and activation of senescent cell anti-apoptotic pathways 
(SCAPs)9,12. Senolytics – compounds that selectively target and deactivate SCAPs to enable 
senescent cell apoptosis12,13 – have demonstrated viable efficacy for suppressing excess vascular 
cell senescence and improving vascular function in old mice6,14,15. Though several senolytic 
interventions exist, the naturally occurring senolytic compound fisetin has emerged as a potent 
senolytic in endothelial cells and a promising candidate for clinical translation14,16,17. 
Importantly, fisetin supplementation is currently being tested in several clinical trials in healthy 
mid-life/older adults and people with chronic disorders. Despite considerable clinical senolytic 
testing underway, the understanding of the mechanisms by which senolytic compounds target 
specific cell types across tissues and the SASP is limited. As such, in the present study, we 
sought to characterize vascular senescent cells and the SASP in the vasculature from young 
(6mo) and old (27mo) mice and leveraged in vivo fisetin supplementation to explore the 
molecular, cellular, and physiological mechanisms modulated with aging and senolytic 
treatment. 
The central goal of this study was to: 1) characterize specific cell types within the 
vasculature that undergo cellular senescence and increased SASP burden with aging; 2) assess 
the role of the circulating SASP milieu in mediating age-related endothelial dysfunction; 3) 
identify candidate factors in the circulating SASP milieu that are altered with aging; and 4) 
determine the causal role of candidate SASP factors in mediating endothelial dysfunction with 
aging. We first uncovered that endothelial cells are the predominant cell type that become 

 
 
136 
senescent in old age and can be effectively eliminated with fisetin supplementation. Within 
senescent endothelial cells, we identified the gene Cxcl12 to be the most highly upregulated in 
old age and ameliorated with fisetin supplementation, and observed similar patterns in 
circulating concentrations of CXCL12 with aging and fisetin supplementation. Finally, we 
revealed a causal role of the circulating SASP milieu in inducing endothelial dysfunction 
mediated in part by CXCL12. Overall, these findings establish senescent endothelial cells as 
promising senolytic targets, demonstrate the SASP as an underlying mechanism of age-related 
endothelial dysfunction, and identify CXCL12 as a specific circulating SASP factor that 
mediates vascular cell senescence and endothelial dysfunction with aging that can be modulated 
with senolytic intervention. 
RESULTS 
 
Whole aortic single-cell RNA sequencing identifies senescent cell population with aging. 
To gain insights into transcriptomic changes that occur with aging, we leveraged single-
cell RNA sequencing (scRNAseq) to compare aortas from young (6mo) and old (27mo) mice. 
scRNAseq workflows allow for the characterization of gene profiles from distinct cell types of 
the aorta to identify cell type-specific transcriptomic changes with aging18. Unsupervised 
clustering of the merged samples was visualized using uniform manifold approximation and 
projection (UMAP) to reveal 29 clusters representing 11 distinct cell types in the aorta (Figure 
1A). Endothelial cells encompassed the largest group of cells (46%) followed by fibroblasts 
(22%), B cells (10%), macrophages (7%), T cells (6%), vascular smooth muscle cells (VSMC; 
4%), neutrophil-like cells (2%), neutrophil cells (1%), erythrocytes (1%), mesothelial cells 
(<1%) and neural cells (<1%) (Figure 1B). Cluster cell type was determined using a 

 
 
137 
combination of the top 50 genes distinguishing the cluster from others and preestablished cell-
specific markers (Figure 1C). 
 
To assess age-related changes in aortic cell populations, we performed cell composition 
analysis between young and old mice (Figure 1D). We found that the endothelial cell proportion 
increased with age (p<0.001) and the T cell proportion decreased with age (p=0.002) (Figure 
1D). Further, we observed trending differences in VSMC (p=0.069), B cells (p=0.109), neural 
(p=0.146), and neutrophil-like (p=0.127) (Figure 1D). These changes in cell populations were 
consistent with age-related morphological changes that occur during intima-media thickening 
and dysregulation of immune cell infiltration into the aorta19. Together, these population changes 
are aligned with physiological changes that occur in the vasculature with aging19,20. 
We next performed whole-aorta pseudo-bulk RNAseq to determine the general 
transcriptomic changes in old vs. young mice. Volcano plot analysis of differentially expressed 
genes (DEGs) in the whole aorta revealed that 5625 genes were upregulated and 2690 genes 
were downregulated with aging (Figure 1E). Reactome pathway analysis revealed cellular 
senescence as the most highly modulated pathway with aging, as well as pathways related to 
immune dysregulation and cell cycle arrest (Figure 1F). Overall, the pseudo-bulk sequencing 
assessment revealed a pro-aging transcriptomic shift in the aortic genes and pathway of old mice 
that is expected with chronological aging. 
Given the changes in cellular senescence pathways observed in aortas from old mice, we 
next sought to identify and characterize senescent cells in the aorta. To do so, we leveraged 
module scoring of the holistic senescent cell signature, SenMayo21, to identify cells expressing 
high levels of cellular senescence and SASP transcripts. We found that SenMayo predicted 

 
 
138 
senescent cell populations in several cell types including endothelial cell, macrophage, and 
neutrophil-like cell populations (Figure 1G). As such, we next sought to characterize the 
senescent cell populations in the endothelial cell population given that: a) it had the largest 
vascular senescent population; b) we observed age-related differences in the population 
composition; and c) it is highly involved in age-related vascular endothelial dysfunction. 
 
Figure 1. Whole aortic single-cell RNA sequencing identifies senescent cell population with 
aging. Uniform manifold approximation and projection (UMAP) (A) and cell type percentage 
(B) of aortic cells from all the samples color-labeled by cell type. Dot plot analysis of cell type-
specific markers used to identify aortic cell types (C). Aortic cell composition by cell type (D) 
and volcano plot representation of differentially expressed gene (E) in old vs. young samples. 
Reactome pathway analysis of whole-aortas upregulated genes (F). Module gene scoring using 
SenMayo signature to identify senescent cells (G). *p<0.05 vs. young; triangles represent 
females, squares represent males.  


 
 
139 
 
Senescent endothelial cells increase in the aorta with aging and are a putative target to 
reverse vascular aging.  
To begin to specify the effects of aging and cellular senescence on the aortic endothelial 
cell population, we merged all endothelial cell clusters and reperformed unsupervised clustering 
which revealed 12 unique endothelial subclusters (Figure 2A). We leveraged the endothelial-
specific senescent signature EndoSen22 to more precisely identify senescent endothelial cells. 
According to EndoSen module scoring, senescent endothelial cells were present in endothelial 
subcluster 4, 7, 9, and 10 with the most intense expression being in subcluster 10 (Figure 2B). 
Moreover, endothelial subcluster 10 had the highest expression of the canonical cellular 
senescence marker Cdkn1a compared to the other subclusters (Figure 2C). As such, we focused 
on age-related changes occurring in this potential senescent subcluster.  
With merged data, we first looked at the top 50 DEGs that distinguished the putative 
senescent subcluster 10 from other endothelial subclusters (Figure 2D). Endothelial subcluster 
10 expressed genes had roles involved in extracellular matrix remodeling, stress signaling, cell 
cycle maintenance, and high levels of SASP factors. We performed pathway analysis on the 
DEGs of senescent endothelial subcluster 10 and found that the identified pathways were related 
to cardiovascular pathology (i.e., atherosclerosis and cardiomyopathy), cellular senescence and 
cancer (which share common pathways), and overall dysregulated signaling pathways (another 
key feature of senescent cells) (Figure 2F). Together, these data suggest an increase of senescent 
endothelial cells with aging that may be a putative target for improving vascular health with 
aging.  

 
 
140 
 
Figure 2. Senescent endothelial cells increase in aortas with aging and are a putative target

reclustered aortic endothelial cells from all the samples (A). Module gene scoring using EndoSen 
signature to identify senescent endothelial cells (B). Violin plot presentation of the canonical

subcluster 10 (D). KEGG pathway analysis of DEGs in endothelial subcluster 10 in old vs. 
young sample (E).  
 
Oral intermittent fisetin supplementation ameliorates senescent vascular cells with aging. 
Given the prevalence of senescent cells in aortas with aging, we next sought to determine 
if we could reduce senescent cell burden using senolytic therapy. Senolytics are compounds that 
selectively eliminate excess senescent cells13,23. Although several senolytic therapies are 
currently being advanced in preclinical and clinical studies, we opted to use the natural senolytic 
fisetin given its efficacy on senescent endothelial cells17, favorable effects on vascular function 
with aging14 and encouraging safety profile for clinical translation16. To study the effects of 
fisetin supplementation on aortic gene expression, we added a group of young and old fisetin-
treated mice to our transcriptomic analysis (Figure 3A). Importantly, we have previously shown 
that oral, intermittent fisetin supplementation eliminates excess senescent cells14. Given that 


 
 
141 
young animals have minimal cellular senescence burden11,24, we included the young fisetin-
treated group to ensure minimal effects of fisetin supplementation in young, healthy animals.  
To assess fisetin-related changes in aortic cell populations, we first performed cell 
composition analysis between the treatment conditions (Figure 3B-C). We found that in young 
mice, fisetin supplementation did not change the cell composition of any of the cell types 
suggesting minimal effects of fisetin on healthy, young animals (Figure 3B-C). In old mice, age-
related differences in endothelial and T cells persisted, even after fisetin supplementation 
(Figure 3B-C). However, fisetin supplementation mitigated trending increases in VSMC 
proportions (p=0.025), suggesting favorable effects on age-related intima-media thickening 
(Figure 3B-C). We next assessed whether fisetin supplementation had favorable effects on 
senescent cell burden in old aortas. Using the SenMayo senescence signature, we performed 
module gene scoring across the treatment conditions. Marginal differences were observed in the 
young groups suggesting minimal effects of fisetin on young, healthy mice (Figure 3D). In 
aortas from old mice, fisetin supplementation appeared to have the greatest effects on the 
endothelial cell population, reinforcing the senolytic effects of fisetin on senescent endothelial 
cells (Figure 3D). To assess the senescent cell genes that were modulated with aging and fisetin 
supplementation, we looked at the SenMayo genes that were upregulated with aging (old vehicle 
vs. young vehicle) and downregulated with fisetin supplementation (old fisetin vs. old vehicle). 
We found that these genes were highly involved in the SASP, cell cycle arrest maintenance, and 
proinflammatory pathways (Figure 3E). Together, these data suggest that fisetin 
supplementation has minimal effects on healthy young mice that have low levels of cellular 
senescence, whereas fisetin supplementation in old mice results in shifts towards young aortic 
cell composition and a reduction in whole-aorta cell senescence.

143 
Fisetin supplementation ameliorates senescent endothelial cells in the aorta with aging. 
We next sought to assess the effects of fisetin supplementation on aortic senescent 
endothelial cells. To identify senescent endothelial cell populations, we used a combination of 
differences in endothelial cell composition and senescent signature module testing. Endothelial 
cell composition revealed endothelial subcluster 10 as a cluster that was upregulated with age 
and downregulated, back towards young levels, in old mice that received fisetin supplementation 
(Figure 4A-B). As validated above, we used the endothelial-specific senescent signature, 
EndoSen, to distinguish senescent populations. Leveraging EndoSen, we found that in the young 
endothelial populations, there was a small number of senescent cells and fisetin supplementation 
had marginal effects on the young tissue, consistent with whole-aorta observations (Figure 4C). 
As detected previously, in old samples, we found senescent cells existed, especially in subcluster 
10 (Figure 4C).  Fisetin supplementation appeared to diminish the subcluster 10 back towards 
intensities observed in the young mice (Figure 4C). In combination, the endothelial cell 
proportions and EndoSen signature implicate endothelial subcluster 10 as a senescent cell 
population targeted by fisetin.  
To further characterize the senescent endothelial subcluster 10, we performed DEG 
testing between groups to identify gene targets that are modulated with age and fisetin 
supplementation. We first compared the young groups and found no genes that were significantly 
changed, suggesting minimal effects of fisetin supplementation on young, healthy endothelial 
cells (Figure 4D). To identify age-related differences, we assessed DEGs in senescent 
endothelial cells between young vs. old vehicle-treated mice. We found that 278 genes were 
upregulated, and 135 genes were downregulated, suggesting a large impact of aging on the 
transcriptome of senescent endothelial subcluster 10 (Figure 4D).  To assess the effects of fisetin 

 
 
144 
supplementation on this subpopulation, we compared subcluster 10 between the old vehicle and 
old fisetin groups. In this comparison we found a greater number of genes to be downregulated 
(177) than upregulated (34), suggesting a shift back towards young levels (Figure 4D). Finally, 
we compared young vehicle vs. old fisetin subcluster 10 to determine if the transcripts were more 
similar to that of the old vehicle. We found that no genes were significantly modulated between 
these groups, suggesting that endothelial cells from old mice that received intermittent fisetin 
supplementation have a similar transcriptomic profile to that of endothelial cells from young 
mice (Figure 4D).   
We next identified the top 50 DEGs that distinguished the old vehicle group from the 
other treatment conditions (Figure 4E). The analysis represents genes that were most 
overexpressed with aging and downregulated with fisetin supplementation in endothelial 
subcluster 10 (Figure 4E). We found that the genes largely encoded canonical senescence 
markers (Cdkn1a) and several SASP factors including Cxcl12, Ccl7, Plat, Ctsd, Serpine2, 
Igfbp5, Tgfb1, Igfbp3, Igfbp4, Fgf1, and Plaur (Figure 4F). In summary, these data reveal that 
fisetin supplementation effectively targets senescent endothelial subcluster 10 and reduces the 
SASP factors expressed by this subpopulation.  
 

 
 
145

of differentially expressed genes (DEGs) between different treatment conditions in endothelial 
subcluster 10 (D). Dot plot analysis of top 50 DEGs between O VEH and other treatment 
conditions (E). Violin plots of the canonical cellular senescence marker, Cdkn1a, and 
senescence-associated secretory phenotype genes across treatment conditions (F). *p<0.0001 vs. 
Y VEH, ^p<0.0001 vs. O VEH; triangle represent females, squares represent males. 
 
Fisetin supplementation mitigates circulating SASP abundance in old mice. 
The SASP represents a collection of pro-inflammatory molecules that have varied roles in 
modulating physiological function8,25. Our next aim was to identify candidate SASP factors that 
may impair endothelial function and be favorably modulated by fisetin supplementation to 
prevent endothelial dysfunction. Provided the changes observed in SASP factors transcripts from

146 
senescent endothelial cells (Figure 4F), we sought to validate that these genes were being 
translated into proteins and secreted into the circulation. We selected SASP proteins to measure 
based on: a) the magnitude of difference in endothelial gene expression of factors that were 
unfavorably modulated with aging and normalized toward young levels with fisetin 
supplementation; and b) for biological plausibility of influencing endothelial function. 
Leveraging the targeted Luminex platform, we measured candidate SASP factors in plasma from 
treated animals including: CCL4, CCL7, CCL8, CXCL12, FGF Basic, GDF15, IGFBP3, PAI1, 
S100A9, TIMP4. Of these factors, no age- or fisetin-related effects were observed in CCL4, 
CCL7, IGFBP3 or TIMP4 abundance. An age-related increase was observed in CCL8, FGF 
Basic, and S100A9 between young vehicle and old vehicle-treated mice. Finally, PAI1 and 
CXCL12 were both higher with aging (old vehicle vs. young vehicle), and this age-related 
increase was ameliorated by fisetin supplementation (old fisetin vs. old vehicle).  
PAI1, a platelet protein that inhibits fibrin degradation26, is a well-established marker of 
cellular senescence27 and validates our senescent cell clearance. CXCL12 is a chemokine that 
regulates angiogenesis28 in the vasculature and is implicated in atherosclerotic progression29. 
Given the novelty and putative biological plausibility of CXCL12 in regulating endothelial 
function (whereas PAI1 has primary effects on platelets), we next sought to investigate its causal 
implication in regulating endothelial function. 

 
 
147

Fisetin supplementation attenuates plasma-induced endothelial dysfunction with aging. 
Endothelial cells line the luminal wall of arteries and have direct contact with the 
circulating milieu, including secreted SASP factors19,30. Given the observation that select 
circulating SASP factors were altered with aging and fisetin supplementation, we next sought to 
assess the functional role of the circulating SASP on age-related endothelial dysfunction and the 
specific effects of CXCL12 as a candidate circulating SASP factor that may be responsible for 
transducing the effect of the circulating milieu on endothelial function. To do so, we leveraged 
our recently developed ex vivo plasma exposure model where isolated carotid arteries from 
young, intervention-naïve mice are exposed ex vivo to sex-matched plasma prior to assessing

148 
endothelium-dependent dilation (EDD)10. Importantly, this model allows us to causally 
determine the effects of the circulating milieu (i.e., the SASP and other bioactive molecules in 
circulation) on endothelial function. In combination, we performed plasma exposure on isolated 
arteries (aortic rings) and cultured human aortic endothelial cells to elucidate underlying 
mechanisms and verify effects directly on endothelial cells. To elucidate the specific role of 
CXCL12 in isolated arteries and endothelial cells, we added recombinant mouse CXCL12 to 
plasma that exhibited low CXCL12 levels (i.e., plasma from young and old fisetin-treated mice). 
Importantly, CXCL12 levels were added back such that the average concentration of CXCL12 
matched that of the plasma from the old vehicle-treated group. In cultured endothelial cells, we 
used the selective neutraligand of CXCL12, LIT-92731,32, to test the effects of CXCL12 
inhibition in plasma with high CXCL12 levels (i.e., old vehicle plasma). Given that we observed 
minimal effects of fisetin supplementation on the gene expression of young mice, we opted to 
only use the young vehicle mice as a young reference group. As such, the functional 
measurements have presented with 3 plasma exposure conditions: young vehicle, old vehicle, 
and old fisetin.   
Circulating SASP- and CXCL12-induced endothelial dysfunction. We first tested the 
effects of the plasma from the 3 identified groups on endothelial function using carotid artery 
EDD following intraluminal plasma perfusion. EDD was measured as a response to increasing 
doses of acetylcholine and is indicative of the endothelial response to the vasodilatory molecule 
nitric oxide (NO). Peak EDD was lower in carotid arteries exposed to plasma from old control 
mice compared to young control mice (old vehicle plasma, 76 ± 3% v. young plasma, 92 ± 1%; 
p<0.0001), suggesting age-related impairments in plasma-induced endothelial function (Figure 
6A). Peak EDD was higher in carotid arteries exposed to plasma from old fisetin-treated mice 

 
 
149 
compared to old vehicle-treated mice (old fisetin plasma, 87 ± 2% v. old vehicle plasma, 76 ± 
3%, p=0.001) (Figure 6A), suggesting that fisetin supplementation prevents plasma-induced 
endothelial dysfunction with aging. The addition of CXCL12 to young plasma impaired peak 
EDD (80 ± 2%) compared to young plasma alone (-12%, p=0.002) (Figure 6A). Likewise, 
CXCL12 added to old fisetin plasma reduced peak EDD (68 ± 3%) compared to old fisetin 
plasma alone (-22%, p<0.0001) and had comparable peak EDD to plasma from old vehicle-
treated mice (-8%, p=0.029) (Figure 6A). No differences in EID were observed between any of 
the groups or conditions (p=0.342), indicating that differences observed in response to plasma 
exposure occurred in an encomium-dependent manner (Figure 6B). Together, these data suggest 
that the circulating SASP milieu directly mediated endothelial dysfunction, and its detrimental 
effects are in part transduced by CXCL12.  
Circulating SASP- and CXCL12-induced vascular cell senescence. One approach by 
which the SASP elicits its adverse effects is through an endocrine induction of cellular 
senescence6,25. As such, to investigate the mechanisms by which the circulating SASP modulates 
endothelial function, we sought to assess SASP-induced senescence by measuring senescent cell 
burden in arteries exposed to plasma. To do so, we measured senescence-associated -
galactosidase (SA--Gal) intensity, which is the most established marker of senescent cell 
burden33,34. We found that when normalized to total cell count there were ~7-fold more SA--
Gal positive cells in aortas exposed to old versus young plasma (p<0.0001) (Figure 6C). SA--
Gal positive cells were ~6-fold lower in aortas exposed to plasma from old fisetin-treated mice 
compared to old control mice (p<0.0001) (Figure 6C).  
 

 
 
150 
Similar to arteries, endothelial cells exposed to old vehicle plasma had 5.6-fold greater 
SA--Gal positive cells than young plasma (p<0.0001) (Figure 6D). SA--Gal positive cells 
were ~2.2-fold lower in aortas exposed to plasma from old fisetin-treated mice compared to old 
control mice (p<0.0001) (Figure 6D). Exogenous CXCL12 induced cellular senescence in 
endothelial cells exposed to young (3.4-fold, p<0.0001) and old fisetin (1.8-fold, p<0.0001) 
plasma (Figure 6D). Further, CXCL12 inhibition with LIT-927 reduced cellular senescence in 
endothelial cells exposed to old vehicle by 1.8-fold (p<0.0001) (Figure 6D).  
In addition to SA--Gal, we confirmed SASP and CXCL12 effects on cellular senescence 
via endothelial gene expression of the cellular senescence biomarkers Cdkn1a and Cdkn2a, 
which encode the cell cycle inhibitor proteins p21 and p16, respectively. We found that HAECs 
exposed to plasma from old vehicle mice had a 45-fold increase in Cdkn1a expression compared 
to young plasma (p<0.0001) and this age-related increase was ameliorated by a 7.5-fold decrease 
compared to old fisetin plasma (p<0.0001 v. old vehicle) (Figure 6E). The addition of CXCL12 
to plasma increased Cdkn1a expression by 12-fold in HAECS exposed to young plasma 
(p<0.0001) and by 2-fold in HAECs exposed to old fisetin plasma (p<0.0001) (Figure 6E). The 
addition of LIT-927 to plasma reduced Cdkn1a expression by 2.4-fold in HAECs exposed to old 
vehicle plasma (p<0.0001) (Figure 6E). Similarly, we found that HAECs exposed to plasma 
from old vehicle mice had a 7.2-fold increase in Cdkn2a expression compared to young plasma 
(p<0.0001) and this age-related increase was ameliorated by a 5.5X-fold decrease compared to 
old fisetin plasma (p<0.0001) (Figure 6F). The addition of CXCL12 to plasma increased 
Cdkn2a expression by 2.5-fold in HAECS exposed to young plasma (p<0.0001) and tended to 
increase expression in HAECs exposed to old fisetin plasma (2.4, p=0.052) (Figure 6F). The 
addition of LIT-927 to plasma reduced Cdkn2a expression by 2.6-fold in HAECs exposed to old 

 
 
151 
vehicle plasma (p<0.0001) (Figure 6F). In combination, these data indicate that the circulating 
SASP milieu directly induces vascular and endothelial cell senescence and that these age-related 
inductions are prevented by fisetin supplementation. Further, CXCL12 partially mediates 
circulating SASP-induced vascular and endothelial cellular senescence.  
Circulating SASP- and CXCL12-induced NO and Superoxide Bioactivity. Age-related 
endothelial dysfunction is primarily mediated by reduced NO bioavailability, which is another 
characteristic of senescent endothelial cells35. As such, we sought to assess NO bioactivity in 
response to acetylcholine in cultured endothelial cells following plasma exposure and CXCL12 
protein manipulation to assess the direct influence of CXCL12 in mediating endothelial NO 
bioactivity. Endothelial cells exposed to old vehicle plasma exhibited 31% reduced NO 
bioactivity compared to young plasma (p<0.0001) (Figure 6G). This age-related NO bioactivity 
impairment was prevented in endothelial cells exposed to old fisetin plasma (+22%, p<0.0001) 
(Figure 6G). Exogenous CXCL12 impaired NO bioactivity in endothelial cells exposed to 
young (-25%, p<0.0001) and old fisetin (-15%, p<0.0001) plasma (Figure 6G). Further, 
CXCL12 inhibition with LIT-927 increased NO bioactivity in endothelial cells exposed to old 
vehicle by 10% (p<0.0001) (Figure 6G).  
Reduced NO bioactivity may occur as a result of excessive superoxide from various 
sources including dysfunctional mitochondria, as excessive superoxide scavenges NO to reduce 
its bioavailability36. As such, we sought to determine whether the circulating SASP induced 
increased mitochondrial superoxide levels and if this manifestation was mediated by CXCL12. 
To do so, we next measured mitochondrial-derived superoxide bioactivity in isolated arteries 
(Figure 6H) and cultured endothelial cells (Figure 6H) following plasma exposure and CXCL12 
protein manipulation. Arterial mitochondrial superoxide levels were higher in aortas exposed to 

 
 
152 
plasma from old control mice compared to young control mice (old vehicle plasma, 8292 ± 823 
arbitrary units [AU] v. young control plasma, 2387 ± 347; p<0.0001) (Figure 6H). 
Mitochondrial superoxide levels were lower in arteries exposed to plasma from old fisetin-
treated mice compared to old vehicle-treated mice (old fisetin plasma, 3443 ± 509 AU v. old 
vehicle plasma, 8294 ± 823 AU, p<0.0001) (Figure 6H). Exogenous CXCL12 induced 
mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity in arteries exposed to old fisetin plasma (7701 ± 982 
p=0.001), but not young plasma (3550 ± 674, p=0.763) (Figure 6H). 
Similar to arteries, endothelial cells exposed to old vehicle plasma had 14% greater 
mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity than young plasma (p<0.0001) (Figure 6I). Mitochondrial 
superoxide bioactivity was 6% lower in endothelial cells exposed to plasma from old fisetin-
treated mice compared to old control mice (p<0.0001) (Figure 6I). Exogenous CXCL12 induced 
mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity in endothelial cells exposed to young plasma (+5%, 
p=0.029), but not old fisetin plasma (Figure 6I). Further, CXCL12 inhibition with LIT-927 
reduced mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity in endothelial cells exposed to old vehicle plasma 
by 10% (p<0.0001) (Figure 6I). Together these data implicate that the circulating SASP milieu 
impairs NO bioactivity and promotes mitochondrial superoxide. Further, these SASP-mediated 
impairments are driven by CXCL12.

Fisetin supplementation mitigates endothelial-to-mesenchymal transition (EndoMT) with 
aging. 
CXCL12 plays a crucial role in promoting ithe EndoMT, a process by which endothelial 
cells lose their characteristic markers, while gaining increased expression of mesenchymal 
markers Importantly, the EndoMT is a putative mechanism of endothelial cell senescence that 
leads to a shift toward contractile properties and reduced NO production and is highly implicated 
in vascular aging. As such, we next sought to determine if the EndoMT is an underlying 
mechanism mediating SASP- and CXCL12-induced endothelial dysfunction in senescent cells. 
As such, we first sought to determine if the senescent endothelial cells demonstrated EndoMT 
attributes and then if fisetin supplementation prevented circulating SASP- and CXCL12-induced 
EndoMT. We first measured cell-specific markers for endothelial cells (Cdh5, Pecam1, Vwf, and 
Nos3) and mesenchymal cells (Tgfb1, Acta2, Vim, and Col1a1) in the senescent endothelial 
subcluster 10 and found a general reduction in endothelial characteristics (Fig. 7A) and increase 
in mesenchymal markers (Fig. 7B) in the old vehicle group compared to the others.  
To determine if the EndoMT is promoted by the circulating SASP milieu and CXCL12 
we exposed HAECs to plasma from the treated mice with and without recombinant mouse 
CXCL12 and LIT-927. HAECs exposed to plasma from old vehicle mice had reduced Cdh5 (0.7-
fold, p<0.0001) and Pecam1 (0.6-fold, p<0.0001) expression compared to young plasma and this 
age-related increase was mitigated by exposure to old fisetin plasma in Cdh5 (3-fold, p<0.0001) 
and Pecam1 (2-fold, p<0.0001) (Fig. 7C-D). The addition of CXCL12 to plasma reduced Cdh5 
(0.4-fold, p<0.0001) and Pecam1 (0.6-fold, p<0.0001) expression in HAECS exposed to young 
plasma and reduced Cdh5 (0.4-fold, p<0.0001) and Pecam1 (0.5-fold, p<0.0001) expression in 
HAECs exposed to old fisetin plasma (Fig. 7C-D). The addition of LIT-927 to old vehicle 

 
 
155 
plasma increased Cdh5 (4-fold, p<0.0001) and Pecam1 (2-fold, p<0.0001) compared to old 
vehicle alone (Fig. 7C-D). Contrary to endothelial markers, HAECs exposed to plasma from old 
vehicle mice had increased Tgfb1 (21-fold, p<0.0001) and Acta2 (8-fold, p<0.0001) expression 
compared to young plasma and this age-related increase was mitigated by exposure to old fisetin 
plasma in Tgfb1 (8-fold, p<0.0001) and Acta2 (9-fold, p<0.0001) (Fig. 7E-F). The addition of 
CXCL12 to plasma increased Tgfb1 (2-fold, p=0.0023) and Acta2 (2-fold, p=0.0001) expression 
in HAECS exposed to young plasma and increased Tgfb1 (5-fold, p<0.0001) and Acta2 (6-fold, 
p<0.0001) expression in HAECs exposed to old fisetin plasma (Fig. 7E-F). The addition of LIT-
927 to old vehicle plasma reduced Tgfb1 (6-fold, p<0.0001) and Acta2 (4-fold, p<0.0001) 
compared to old vehicle alone (Fig. 7E-F). In combination, these data indicate that the 
circulating SASP milieu and CXCL12 promote EndoMT in senescent endothelial cells and that 
these age-related inductions are prevented by fisetin supplementation.

DISCUSSION  
In the present study, we characterized vascular senescent cells, the vascular SASP profile, 
and the circulating SASP milieu in the context of aging. Our findings demonstrate that 
endothelial cells are particularly susceptible to cellular senescence with aging and that fisetin, a 
naturally occurring senolytic compound, effectively eliminates senescent endothelial cells in old 
mice. Leveraging differences observed in senescent endothelial cell transcription, we establish 
the circulating SASP milieu as an underlying mechanism of age-related endothelial dysfunction 
and demonstrate the causal role of the SASP factor CXCL12 in mediating impaired endothelial 
function. These results contribute to a growing body of literature supporting the role of senescent 
cells and the SASP in vascular aging and the potential of senolytic therapy to mitigate age-
related vascular dysfunction. However, these results go beyond descriptive analyses to reveal a 
causal role of vascular senescent cells, the circulating SASP milieu, and CXCL12 in mediating 
age-related endothelial dysfunction.   
Vascular cell senescence and SASP.  
Cellular senescence and the SASP increase with advancing age in the vasculature6,11 and 
have previously been characterized in aortic samples from mice24, primates37, and humans38. 
Senescent cells accumulate in the vasculature among various mammals and promote chronic, 
low-grade inflammation and excess oxidative stress through the SASP39,40. As such, senescent 
cells and the SASP significantly contribute to CVDs including atherosclerosis41, hypertension42, 
and occlusive stroke43. Previous studies utilizing senolytic treatments have demonstrated 
improvements in vascular function6,14 leading to an amelioration of CVD progression15,44 
following excess senescent cell clearance. Here, we extend these observations by demonstrating 
the effects of senolytic intervention on specific senescent cell populations in the vasculature.  

 
 
158 
In old mouse aortas, our senescent cell characterization identified endothelial, 
macrophage and neutrophil-like cells to have high cellular senescence signatures with aging. 
Further, changes in cell composition demonstrated higher endothelial and lower T cell 
populations with aging. Given the increase in endothelial cell senescence burden, age-related 
increase in endothelial population composition, and robust contributions to vascular function, we 
further characterized the effects of senolytic treatment on endothelial cell senescence. Previous 
studies have demonstrated that fisetin reduces vascular cell senescence in vivo14 and in senescent 
endothelial cells in vitro14,17. We extend these findings by demonstrating that in vivo fisetin 
supplementation suppresses cellular senescence in endothelial cells that exhibit high senescent 
cell signatures. Importantly, the senescent endothelial cell population that we identified was 
involved in pathways related to cardiovascular pathologies and revealed a high expression of 
SASP-related transcripts. As such, our findings align with previous studies reporting the efficacy 
of senolytic agents in reducing senescent cell burden in aging tissues. However, this study 
extends prior work by identifying specific vascular cell populations targeted by senolytic 
intervention. 
Circulating SASP-mediated endothelial dysfunction. 
Vascular endothelial dysfunction is a key manifestation of age-related vascular 
dysfunction and a major antecedent to overt CVDs, including atherosclerosis and occlusive 
stroke2,45. Excessive levels of cellular senescence impair endothelial function6 and contribute to 
other endothelium-related manifestations of  vascular dysfunction manifestations including 
arterial stiffening6 and impaired angiogenesis46. The aged circulating milieu is a key driver of 
age-related endothelial dysfunction10; however, the specific component(s) of the circulation 
responsible for transducing impairments in endothelial function remains to be elucidated. One of 

 
 
159 
the key discoveries in the present study is the establishment of the circulating SASP milieu as an 
underlying mechanism of age-related endothelial dysfunction. Our plasma exposure experiments 
in isolated arteries and cultured endothelial cells established a causal role for the aged circulating 
SASP milieu in impairing endothelial function. Age-associated endothelial dysfunction is largely 
mediated by the excess accumulation of senescent cells and the associated circulating SASP 
milieu6,15. The circulating SASP can also induce cellular senescence in neighboring cells, further 
promoting tissue-level dysfunction8. Senescent endothelial cells also contain dysfunctional 
mitochondria which produce high levels of superoxide36,47 and are key regulators of the SASP48. 
In turn, senescent endothelial cells demonstrate deficient NO bioactivity due in part to increased 
superoxide which sequesters NO, reducing its bioavailability, and limits NO production through 
the uncoupling of endothelial NO synthase35. Here, we showed that the exposure of young 
arteries and proliferating endothelial cells to the aged circulating SASP milieu could induce 
paracrine senescence, reduce NO bioactivity, and stimulate excessive mitochondrial superoxide-
related oxidative stress. Conversely, these age-related impairments induced by the aged 
circulating SASP milieu were at least in part prevented when exposed to the circulating milieu 
from old animals that received fisetin supplementation. Collectively, these findings establish the 
SASP as an underlying mechanism by which the circulating milieu drives endothelial 
dysfunction with aging. 
CXCL12-mediated endothelial dysfunction. 
We next sought to determine candidate factors in the circulating SASP milieu that may be 
responsible for transducing endothelial dysfunction with aging, as identification of these 
molecules would provide insight into novel mechanisms by which cellular senescence drives 
endothelial dysfunction with aging and potentially establish novel therapeutic targets. The 

 
 
160 
chemokine CXCL12 emerged from this study as a factor that was most highly upregulated in 
senescent endothelial cells with age, with its expression reduced following fisetin 
supplementation. Additionally, CXCL12 protein levels were elevated in the plasma of aged 
animals and restored to young levels by fisetin supplementation. Given that CXCL12 has been 
implicated in inflammatory29,49 and cellular senescence pathways21,22, its role as a SASP factor in 
vascular aging provides mechanistic insight into how endothelial dysfunction develops with 
aging. CXCL12, through its interaction with the receptor CXCR4, controls the transduction of 
downstream signaling pathways involved in the endothelial-to-mesenchymal transition 
(EndoMT), a process where endothelial cells gain enhanced contractile properties 28,29,49. The 
manipulation of CXCL12 levels in our plasma exposure experiments supports a causal role for 
CXCL12 in age-related endothelial dysfunction, as inhibition of CXCL12 improved endothelial 
responses, whereas exogenous CXCL12 exposure recapitulated age-related dysfunction, 
suggesting fisetin supplementation mitigated circulating SASP milieu-related endothelial 
dysfunction, in part, by targeting CXCL12. Like the SASP milieu, circulating CXCL12 
independently induced paracrine senescence, reduced NO bioactivity, and stimulated excess 
mitochondrial oxidative stress to confer dysfunction in isolated arteries and cultured endothelial 
cells. Although endothelial cell-derived CXCL12 has been identified to promote atherosclerosis 
progression, our findings also demonstrate that CXCL12 promotes endothelial dysfunction 
through the EndoMT, a manifestation that is prevented by fisetin supplementation. The ability of 
fisetin to ameliorate CXCL12-driven endothelial dysfunction highlights its therapeutic potential 
in mitigating vascular aging and establishes CXCL12 as a novel therapeutic target to mitigate 
vascular cell senescence.  
Experimental considerations.  

 
 
161 
Despite these promising findings, particular experimental considerations should be noted. 
First, while our study establishes a link between CXCL12 and endothelial senescence, additional 
mechanistic studies are needed to determine the precise pathways through which CXCL12 
contributes to vascular aging. Additionally, although fisetin effectively reduced CXCL12 levels 
and improved endothelial function in our mouse model, the translatability of these findings to 
humans remains to be fully explored. Ongoing clinical trials investigating fisetin 
supplementation in mid-life and older adults will provide further insights into its efficacy and 
safety in human populations. Although there were no distinct sex differences in the present 
study, sexual dimorphism in vascular aging and senescence warrants further exploration. 
Investigating sex-specific responses to fisetin treatment will be crucial in optimizing therapeutic 
strategies for both men and women. 
CONCLUSIONS 
 
In conclusion, this study identifies that senescent endothelial cells accumulate with 
advancing age and promote the systemic SASP program. Senolytic intervention with fisetin 
effectively eliminated senescent endothelial cells and highlighted CXCL12 as a critical 
endothelium-derived SASP factor that is modulated with age and senolytic treatment. 
Mechanistically, we uncovered that the circulating SASP milieu and CXCL12 induce cellular 
senescence, reduce NO bioactivity, and promote mitochondrial oxidative stress to confer 
endothelial dysfunction. By demonstrating that fisetin effectively reduces CXCL12 levels and 
improves endothelial function, our findings provide mechanistic insight into the therapeutic 
potential of senolytic compounds in vascular aging. These results may have important 
implications for ongoing and future clinical studies aimed at developing senolytic therapies to 
improve cardiovascular health in aging populations. 

 
 
162 
METHODS 
 
Animals and experimental design. 
Male and female C57Bl/6N (wildtype) mice were obtained from the National Institute of 
Aging colony (maintained by Charles River, Wilmington, MA). For the duration of the study, all 
mice were single housed at the National Institute of Aging animal facility with a 12hr:12hr light-
dark cycle and allowed ad libitum access to an irradiated, fixed, and open rodent chow 
(Inotiv/Envigo 7917, stored at room temperature). 
For the intervention period, young (6 months) and old (27 months) wildtype mice were 
randomly assigned to receive vehicle (10% ethanol, 30% PEG400 and 60% Phosal 50 PG) or 
fisetin (100 mg/kg/day in vehicle). For the young mice, 12 mice (6M/6F) received the vehicle 
and 12 mice (6M/6F) received fisetin, and for old mice, 8 mice (3M/5F) received vehicle and 10 
mice (4M/6F) received fisetin. Treatment was administered via oral gavage using an intermittent 
dosing paradigm – one week on; two weeks off; one week on, as previously established senolytic 
dosing paradigm14,16. Mice were sacrificed one to two weeks following the final dose to rule out 
any acute effects of the compound as the terminal half-life of fisetin is ~3.1 hours in plasma50.  
Aortic single-cell RNA sequencing. 
Aortic single-cell transcriptomic analysis was performed, as previously described44. 
Briefly, mice were anesthetized with ketamine and xylazine and blood was extracted using 
cardiac puncture. The whole aorta was collected after left ventricular perfusion with 10 ml of 
PBS and quickly transferred to cold PBS. To prepare a single-cell suspension, perivascular 
adipose tissue was removed and whole aortas from three mice (per sex and treatment group) 
were cut into ~ 1-mm pieces and digested with an enzyme solution consisting of 10 mg/ml 
Collagenase II (Gibco) and 1 mg/ml elastase (Worthington Biochemical Corp.) in Dulbecco's 

 
 
163 
Modified Eagle Medium (DMEM; 3 ml/sample) for 15 min at 37°C in a tissue culture incubator. 
The cell suspension was strained through a 40-µm filter and centrifuged at 500 x g for 5 min. 
Cells were resuspended in DMEM supplemented with 15% fetal bovine serum (FBS) and 5 mM 
of EDTA and then stained with 2 µg/ml of propidium iodide for 5 min to assess cell viability and 
single, viable cells were sorted using Fluorescence-Activated Cell Sorting (FACS) Aria FUSION 
(BD Bioscience) for 10x Genomics library preparation. The single-cell libraries were prepared 
with Chromium Next GEM Single Cell 3' Kit v3.11 (10x Genomics) with Chromium Next GEM 
Chip G Single Cell Kit (10x Genomics) according to manufacturer’s protocol with Chromium 
Controller (10x Genomics). Briefly, ~10,000 single cells were used for GEM generation, and the 
cDNAs were then prepared checked on the Agilent Bioanalyzer with High Sensitivity DNA kit 
(Agilent). Prepared cDNAs were then used for library preparation that were checked on the 
Agilent Bioanalyzer with DNA 1000 kit (Agilent Technologies). The libraries were sequenced 
with Illumina NovaSeq 6000 sequencer at a depth of 70,000-120,000 reads per cell. 
Single-cell RNA sequencing was processed following the Feature Barcoding tool pipeline 
in Cell Ranger (version 9.0.0; 10x Genomics) and the mouse mm10 reference genome. The 
obtained read count matrices were subsequently analyzed in R using the Seurat package, version 
5.2.0 with default parameters in all functions, unless otherwise specified. Standard quality 
control filtering was applied to each sample to eliminate low-quality cells and potential doublets 
from downstream analysis. Filtering also removed cells containing more than 25% mitochondrial 
RNAs, expressing fewer than 200 or greater than 7,000. RNAs that were detected in less than 3 
cells were excluded from the analysis. For each sample, over 90% of filtered cells remained and 
RNA data was analyzed a with “LogNormalize” method followed by running the 
FindVariableFeatures function to select 2,000 most variable RNAs for dimensionality reduction. 

 
 
164 
The FindIntegrationAnchors function was applied to choose anchors for data integration. After 
performing Principal Component Analysis (PCA), the top 40 PCA dimensions were determined 
by the ElbowPlot method and used to create the Uniform Manifold Approximation and 
Projection (UMAP) with the resolution parameter set to 0.3 resulting in 29 clusters. 
Differentially expressed marker RNAs for each cluster were identified with the FindAllMarkers 
function and the FindMarkers function was used to find differentially expressed genes between 
experimental conditions. The main cell types were identified using marker genes of each cluster 
in combination with data from literature. To identify subclusters within the cell types, the 
analysis was rerun separately on the cells of each cluster and the UMAPs were created using the 
resolution 0.3. Senescent cells assessment scoring was performed with Seurat function 
AddModuleScore. Pathway analysis was performed using KEGG. 
Plasma protein quantification.  
For multiplex analysis, plasma was thawed and centrifuged at 16,000 x g for 4 min. 
Custom murine Luminex Assay kits were designed by R&D Biosystems to include the following 
analytes: CCL4, CCL7, CCL8, CXCL12, FGF Basic, GDF15, IGFBP3, PAI1, S100A9, TIMP4. 
Plasma was diluted 1:10 using the Calibrator Diluent RD6-52 provided in the kit. Standards 
(provided with the kit), blanks, and plasma were incubated with the microparticle cocktail for 2 h 
at 25°C, followed by incubation with Biotin-Antibody cocktail for 1 h. The final incubation 
lasted 30 min with Streptavidin-PE and shaking at 25°C prior to running the plate on the Bio-
Rad Bioplex-200 Instrument. Each incubation was followed by washing 3 times with Wash 
Buffer (provided in the kit). Instrument settings were adjusted to the following: 50 µl sample 
volume, Bio-Plex 

 
 
165 
MagPlex Beads (Magnetic), Double Discriminator Gates set at 8,000 and 23,000, low RP1 target 
value for the CAL2 setting, 50 count/region. The results were analyzed with the Bio-Plex 
Manager software. 
Plasma-mediated vascular endothelial function. 
To assess the role of the circulating SASP milieu (plasma) and CXCL12 on endothelial 
function, an ex vivo isolated artery model was leveraged, as previously described10. In brief, 
carotid arteries were excised from young (3-6 month), intervention-naïve wildtype mice and 
cannulated onto pressure myographs. Plasma collected from young vehicle, old vehicle and old-
fisetin treated mice was diluted in a solution containing 5% sex-matched plasma, 1% penicillin-
streptomycin antibiotic cocktail, and 94% modified Krebs buffer corrected to pH 7.3. The diluted 
plasma samples were perfused luminally through the pressurized arteries for 24h prior to 
assessing endothelial function. Recombinant mouse CXCL12 protein was added back to plasma 
with low CXCL12 levels (e.g., plasma from young and old fisetin-treated mice), such that 
concentration of CXCL12 matched the average levels in old vehicle-treated mice (3210 pg/ml). 
Following plasma perfusion, endothelial function was measured endothelium-dependent dilation 
(EDD) and endothelium-independent dilation (EID) in response to increasing doses of 
acetylcholine (ACh) and sodium nitroprusside (SNP), respectively, as described previously. In 
brief, after vessels were pre-constricted with phenylephrine (PE; 2 mM; Sigma-Aldrich), EDD 
was assessed by measuring increases in luminal diameter in response to increasing 
concentrations of ACh (1 X 10-9 to 1 X 10-4 M; Sigma-Aldrich). Following EDD, EID was 
assessed by measuring the increase in luminal diameter in response to increasing concentrations 
of SNP, an exogenous NO donor (1 X 10-10 to 1 X 10-4 M; Sigma-Aldrich). All dose response 

 
 
166 
data are presented as percent dilation relative to maximum diameter to account for differences in 
baseline vessel diameter. 
Arterial mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity. 
Arterial mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity was assessed using the mitochondrial-
specific superoxide spin probe 1-hydroxy-4-[2-triphenylphosphonio-acetamido]-2,2,6,6-
tetramethylpiperidine (mitoTEMPO-H; Enzo Life Sciences,) by electron paramagnetic resonance 
(EPR) spectrometry, as previously described6,14. In short, 1 mm aortic rings were incubated in 
5% sex-matched plasma from young, old vehicle- or old fisetin-treated mice in DMEM for 24h. 
Recombinant mouse CXCL12 protein was added back to plasma from young and old fisetin-
treated mice, such that concentration of CXCL12 matched the average levels in old vehicle-
treated mice (3210 pg/ml) and 1 µg/ml of LIT-927 was added to the old vehicle plasma31. 
Following plasma exposure, aortic rings incubated in Krebs/HEPES buffer containing 0.5 mM 
mitoTEMPO-H at 37°C for 1 h. Samples were analyzed using aMS300 Xband EPR spectrometer 
(Magnettech). 
Cultured endothelial cell nitric oxide (NO) and mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity. 
Human aortic endothelial cells (HAECs) (PromoCell; used at passag 3-4; female, age: 80 
years, non-smoker, free from known CVD) were cultured in a 96-well glass bottom plate 
(CellVis) under standard culture conditions (37.5°C, 100% relative humidity, 5% CO2). HAECs 
were grown in basal media (Endothelial Cell Growth Medium-2 [EGM-2] BulletKit; PromoCell) 
supplemented with 5% plasma collected young, old vehicle, and old fisetin-treated mice for 2 h. 
Recombinant mouse CXCL12 protein was added back to plasma from young and old fisetin-
treated mice, such that concentration of CXCL12 matched the average levels in old vehicle-
treated mice (3210 pg/ml) and 1 µg/ml of LIT-927 was added to the old vehicle plasma31. 

 
 
167 
HAECs grown in basal media and FBS were used as a control condition. HAECs were stained 
with the fluorescent probe Hoechst (nuclei stain; Thermo Fisher) and either 10 µM 
diaminorhodamine-4M AM (DAR-4M AM; Sigma-Aldrich) (to quantify NO production) for 45 
min or 5 µM MitoSOX (Thermo Fisher) (to quantify mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity) for 
30 min. Live HAECs were imaged at 20x using wide-field fluorescence microscopy (EVOS 
M7000 Imaging System; Thermo Fisher) under standard incubation conditions. HAECs stained 
with DAR-4M AM were imaged before and 6 min after addition of 100 µM acetycholine 
(Sigma) to stimulate NO production51 and mitochondrial superoxide bioactivity was assessed 
under basal (unstimulated) conditions52. Images were quantified using Celleste 5.0 Image 
Analysis Software (Thermo Fisher) as previously described51,52 and normalized to the FBS 
condition on each plate to control for potential variation between plates.  
Senescence-associated β-galactosidase (SA-β-gal) staining. 
Arterial and cultured endothelial cell SA-β-gal staining was performed using the 
Senescence Detection Kit (Abcam) according to the manufacturer’s instructions. Briefly, 1 mm 
aortic rings or HAECs (described above) were incubated in 5% sex-matched plasma from young, 
old vehicle- or old fisetin-treated mice in DMEM or EGM-2, respectively, for 24 h. Recombinant 
mouse CXCL12 protein was added back to plasma from young and old fisetin-treated mice, such 
that concentration of CXCL12 matched the average levels in old vehicle-treated mice (3210 
pg/ml) and 1 µg/ml of LIT-927 was added to the old vehicle plasma31. Following plasma 
exposure, aortas and HAECs were washed with PBS, fixed (fixing solution provided in kit), and 
incubated with the X-gal solution overnight. Aortic rings were then washed, frozen in OCT 
compound, and stored at -80°C until the time of sectioning. Aortic samples were later sectioned 
(7 µm; Leica CM300, Leica Biosystems) and plated in poly-L-lysine coated slides. Aortas and 

 
 
168 
HAECs were stained with DAPI overnight and images were captured using bright-field and 
fluorescent microscopy (EVOS M7000 Imaging System; Thermo Fisher) at 10X magnification 
and quantified using ImageJ, as described 33. 
Cultured endothelial cell gene expression.  
mRNA gene expression was measured in HAECs following incubation with 5% plasma 
from young, old vehicle or old fisetin-treated mice cultured in EGM-2 for 24 h. Recombinant 
mouse CXCL12 protein was added back to plasma from young and old fisetin-treated mice, such 
that concentration of CXCL12 matched the average levels in old vehicle-treated mice (3210 
pg/ml) and 1 µg/ml of LIT-927 was added to the old vehicle plasma31. Briefly, RNA was 
extracted using the RNeasy mini kit (Qiagen). cDNA was synthesized using the iScript cDNA 
synthesis kit (Bio-Rad Laboratories). Transcripts of cellular senescence markers, Cdkn2a and 
Cdkn1a were analyzed using a StepOnePlus Real-Time PCR System (Applied Biosystems) in 
96-well plates and the Taqman OpenArray (Applied Biosystems) was used as a master mix, as 
described. SimpleSeq DNA sequencing (Quintara Biosciences) was used to validate PCR 
products.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

 
 
169
