174 
CHAPTER V 
 
CONCLUSIONS 
 
The purpose of this dissertation was to investigate the natural senolytic compound fisetin 
on its ability to suppress excess vascular cell senescence and the senescence-associated secretory 
phenotype (SASP), mechanisms-of-action, and improve age-related vascular dysfunction. 
Despite advancements in senotherapeutics drug development, research investigating age-related 
changes in cellular senescence and the SASP has not conclusively determined whether these 
differences are causally implicated in vascular dysfunction or feasible for clinical translation. In 
Chapter II, I analyzed scientific literature pertaining to mechanisms by senescent cells and the 
SASP elicit their detrimental effects on vascular function. I reviewed evidence of 
senotherapeutic intervention on vascular function and sought to explore clinical groups that 
exhibit high senescent cell burden and early vascular dysfunction and may stand to benefit the 
most from senotherapeutic treatment. Despite the promise of senotherapeutics, I also highlighted 
the potential disadvantageous effects of senescent cell clearance especially in vascular diseases 
emphasizing the need to further understand the role of senescent cells in vascular dysfunction 
and disease. 
In Chapter III, I sought to test the senolytic effects of fisetin supplementation on the 
vasculature. I first identified optimal concentrations of fisetin that would eliminate senescent, but 
not proliferating endothelial cells in culture. Translating to old mice, I found that late-life fisetin 
supplementation reduced vascular cell senescence and improved age-related arterial stiffness and 
endothelial function. These improvements in vascular function were mediated by a suppression 
of excess cellular senescence, improvement in nitric oxide (NO) bioavailability, a reduction in 
whole-cell and mitochondrial-specific oxidative stress, and favorable arterial wall remodeling. 

 
 
175 
These findings provided initial evidence that fisetin may be an effective senolytic therapy in the 
vasculature and holds promise for clinical translation to healthy mid-life and old adults. 
In alignment with this observation, in chapter IV, I sought to characterize senescent cells 
and the SASP in the vasculature to better understand the cellular-molecular mechanisms by 
which vascular senescent cells change with aging and senolytic intervention. Using aortic single-
cell transcriptomic techniques, I identified that endothelial cells composition increased and were 
highly susceptible to becoming senescent in the context of aging. Fisetin supplementation was 
used to investigate the cellular-molecular changes that occur with senolytic intervention in the 
aorta and demonstrated robust effects on senescent endothelial cell populations. Senescent 
endothelial cells exhibited pathways related to cardiovascular pathology and highly expressed 
SASP factors. Of the expressed SASP factors, the Cxcl12 gene expression was the most highly 
upregulated with age and downregulated with senolytic treatment and this pattern persisted in 
circulating CXCL12 protein levels in plasma. As such, we next sought to determine the role of 
the circulating SASP milieu and CXCL12 in regulating endothelial function. Ex vivo isolated 
artery and endothelial cell culture exposure to plasma from treated mice revealed that the aged 
circulating SASP milieu directly induced endothelial dysfunction through cellular senescence 
induction, reduced NO bioactivity, and increased mitochondrial oxidative stress. These 
circulating SASP-mediated impairments were in part transduced by CXCL12 and prevented by 
senolytic intervention. These findings establish the circulating SASP milieu as an underlying 
mechanism of endothelial dysfunction and identify CXCL12 as a mediator and therapeutic target 
of cellular senescence-induced endothelial dysfunction.  
Collectively, these dissertation studies identify cellular senescence and the SASP as 
mechanisms of age-related vascular dysfunction, offering insight into potential preventative and 

 
 
176 
therapeutic targets. Senotherapeutic, particularly fisetin, emerged as promising strategies to 
target senescent cells and the SASP and mitigate cardiovascular disease risk associated with 
aging. 
FUTURE DIRECTIONS 
 
With the growing number of older adults there is a pressing need for effective therapies 
to reduce the risk and prevalence of cardiovascular diseases. These dissertation studies have 
identified the accumulation of cellular senescence and the SASP as mechanisms by which aging 
induces vascular dysfunction. As such, targeting excess cellular senescence and the SASP may 
be an effective approach for improving vascular function and reducing risk of vascular-related 
clinical disorders of aging. Notably, based on the findings presented in this dissertation in mice, a 
randomized, placebo-controlled trial is currently being conducted by my co-mentor and 
dissertation committee member Dr. Matthew Rossman to determine the efficacy of fisetin 
supplementation for reducing vascular cell senescence and improving vascular function in 
healthy mid-life and older adults (NCT06133634).  
To propel cellular senescence, SASP, and senolytic research forward, there is a need to 
move beyond descriptive analyses and develop mechanistic understandings of microbial 
influences in humans. Excitingly, new approaches are being pioneered to do just that. Novel 
biomarkers of cellular senescence, the SASP, and senescent cell clearance are emerging to better 
quantify burden and clearance. Preclinical transgenic models are being advanced to have abilities 
to perform cell type- or tissue-specific senescent cell clearance which will allow for the 
investigation of the role of cellular senescence from certain regions and influence on other 
tissues and systems. The recent development of our laboratory’s circulating milieu bioassays 
allows for the causal investigation of components and specific factors of the bloodstream, 

 
 
177 
including the circulating SASP milieu and SASP factors. Finally, new senolytic compounds are 
being developed with greater potency, specificity, and safety profiles that may have high 
implications in clinical medicine.  
Overall, as the field progresses towards deeper mechanistic insights via the continued 
understanding of molecular underpinnings of senescent cells, tools to measure and test the role of 
cellular senescence and the SASP and development of cellular senescence- and SASP-targeted 
therapeutic strategies that will impact vascular function and mitigate vascular-related disorders 
during aging. This collective pursuit promises to advance our understanding and pave the way 
for transformative therapeutic strategies in combating cardiovascular diseases among mid-life 
and older adults.
